When a user selects " Yes, and don't ask again ," Anthropic's AI coding assistant can save the command, word-for-word, including the secret, in a project settings file.   Software security company Apiiro said yesterday that 1,122 public copies of that file hold at least one secret, though it did not test whether any still work. One company the researchers notified earlier this week confirmed its key had been live.   The file is .claude/settings.local.json. Removing it from a project does not undo the leak, because older versions stay in git history and in forks of the repository. The researchers' advice is to rotate first: change every password, token, and key the file has ever held, then clean up. The update that installs the fix still unpacks its files using the site's old, unpatched code, so an attacker who strikes at the right moment can run code on the server. The researcher advises blocking web access to the site's wp-content/upgrade folder before updating.   The flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-119977 , affects WordPress 4.7 through 7.1.3. During a core update that uses a complete package, WordPress unpacks a copy of the new version into a temporary folder inside wp-content/upgrade. That copy includes the WordPress installer, and the web server can usually reach it.   An attacker with no account who can determine the folder's name and access the installer before WordPress deletes the copy can end up running code on the server. The issue, discovered  by security researcher Ozgun Kultekin, has been addressed in OpenSSH 10.6p2 . The flaw that makes this possible can still be exploited, the researchers said earlier this week.   Claude Desktop is an AI agent that can run commands on the computer where it is installed. The researchers found that Dispatch also accepted an ordinary signed-in session on claude.ai, the Claude website, even though the site showed no link to it. That let an extension running on the site send Claude Desktop instructions as if the user had sent them. I agree with him. Go read it if you haven't. I've spent the summer looking at what these models find, and the gap he describes between what they can do and what the rest of us are ready for is the one I've been staring at since May.   The hardest piece of his plan is the one that asks governments to coordinate. They're in a difficult spot: regulate too little and a US company accidentally builds the weapon, regulate too much and it gets built somewhere else, and open source isn't governable either way. Where governments can act is on consumption: not what the models can do, but what happens to what they find. They need network access to the server, an account name, and a password between 257 and 1,024 bytes long.   OpenSSH, which rates the flaw critical, released version 10.6p2 today to block it. Alpine has published fixed packages for every release back to 3.16.   The attack works when the OpenSSH server (sshd) checks passwords without the Pluggable Authentication Module (PAM) interface, password logins are enabled, and at least one other account on the machine has a password. Alpine's openssh-server package is built without PAM, and password logins are on by default.   The flaw comes from how sshd works with musl, the C library that Alpine is built on. Quite a lot, it seems. A stolen login can open doors across several systems. A trusted setting can be used to lock people out. Even a simple file copy can reach places it shouldn't. And while new tools are helping find security holes faster, some important systems are still taking months, sometimes over a year, to get patched. Not exactly a great match.   The stranger part is how little some attacks now need. Ransom demands without encrypted files. Fake websites that look real enough to steal money. AI tools picking up dangerous instructions while trying to help. Even the attackers are making mistakes in their own code.
